Appendix A
Proofs

A.1
Proof of Proposition
8
(Bayes-optimal Classifier for Ratio-of-linear
\psi
)

Proposition

((Restated) Bayes optimal classifier for ratio-of-linear
\psi
)
.

Let the performance measure
\psi:[0,1]^{d}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
in
OP1
be of the form
\psi({\mathbf{C}})=\frac{\langle{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle}{\langle{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle}
for some
{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{B}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with
\langle{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle>0~\forall{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
.
Let
t^{*}=\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}})
and

{\mathbf{L}}^{*}={\mathbf{A}}-t^{*}{\mathbf{B}}
.
Then any classifier that is optimal for the linear metric
\langle{\mathbf{L}}^{*},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle
is also optimal for
OP1
.

We will find the following lemma useful in the proof of the proposition.

Lemma 25
.

Let
\psi:[0,1]^{d}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be such that
\psi({\mathbf{C}})=\frac{\langle{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle}{\langle{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle},

for some matrices
{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{B}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with
\langle{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle>0
for all
{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
. Let
t^{*}=\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}})
. Then
\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\langle{\mathbf{A}}-t^{*}{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle=0.

{proof}

Define
\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{R}}
as
\varphi(t)=\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\langle{\mathbf{A}}-t{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle.
It is easy to see that
\varphi
(being a point-wise supremum of linear functions) is convex, and hence a continuous function over
{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
t^{*}=\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}})
.
We then have for all
{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\langle{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle}{\langle{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle}\,\geq\,t^{*}~~~\text{or equivalently}~~~\varphi(t^{*})\,=\,\langle{\mathbf{A}}-t^{*}{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle\,\geq\,0.

Thus

\displaystyle\varphi(t^{*})=\sup_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\langle{\mathbf{A}}-t^{*}{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle\geq 0\;.

(10)

Also, by continuity of
\frac{\langle{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle}{\langle{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle}
in
{\mathbf{C}}
, for any
t<t^{*}
, there exists
{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
such that

\displaystyle\frac{\langle{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle}{\langle{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle}\,>\,t~~~\text{or equivalently}~~~\varphi(t)\,=\,\langle{\mathbf{A}}-t{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle\,<\,0.

Thus for all
t<t^{*}
,

\varphi(t)=\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\langle{\mathbf{A}}-t{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle<0\;.

Next, by continuity of
\varphi
, for any monotonically increasing sequence of real numbers
\{t_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
converging to
t^{*}
, we have that
\varphi(t_{i})
converges to
\varphi(t^{*})
; since for each
t_{i}
in this sequence
\varphi(t_{i})<0
, at the
t^{*}
, we have that
\varphi(t^{*})\geq 0
. Along with (
10
), this gives us

\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\langle{\mathbf{A}}-t^{*}{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle=\varphi(t^{*})=0,

as desired.

We are now ready to prove Proposition
8
.

{proof}
[Proof of Proposition
8
]
Let
{h}^{*}:{\mathcal{X}}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{n}
be a classifier that is optimal for
{\mathbf{L}}^{*}={\mathbf{A}}-t^{*}{\mathbf{B}}
, i.e.,

\langle{\mathbf{A}}-t^{*}{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}[{h}^{*}]\rangle\,=\,\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\langle{\mathbf{A}}-t^{*}{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle.

Note from Lemma
25
that
\langle{\mathbf{A}}-t^{*}{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}[{h}^{*}]\rangle=0
. Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{\langle{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{C}}[{h}^{*}]\rangle}{\langle{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}[{h}^{*}]\rangle}\,=\,t^{*},\text{or equivalently},~~~~\psi({\mathbf{C}}[{h}^{*}])\,=\,\inf_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}}),

which shows that
h^{*}
is also
\psi
-optimal.

A.2
Proof of Proposition
9
(Bayes-optimal Classifier for Monotonic
\psi
)

Proposition

((Restated) Bayes-optimal classifier for monotonic
\psi
)
.

Let the performance measure
\psi:[0,1]^{d}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
in
OP1
be differentiable and bounded, and be monotonically decreasing in
C_{ii}
for each
i
and non-decreasing in
C_{ij}
for all
i,j
. Let
{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(X)

be a continuous random vector.
Then there exists a loss matrix
{\mathbf{L}}^{*}
(which depends on
\psi
and
D
) such that any classifier that is optimal for the linear metric given by
{\mathbf{L}}^{*}
is also optimal for
OP1
.

Let
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
denote the closure of
{\mathcal{C}}
.
We will find the following lemma crucial to our proof.

Lemma 26
.

Let
\eta(X)
be a continuous random vector.
Let
{\mathbf{L}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be such that no two columns are identical. Then,

\operatorname{argmin}_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}\,\langle{\mathbf{L}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle\,=\,\operatorname{argmin}_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\langle{\mathbf{L}},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle.

Moreover, the above set is a singleton.

The proof for the lemma is highly technical and can be found in the conference version of the paper, specifically Lemma 12 in
Narasimhan et al. 2015b
.

{proof}

[Proof of Proposition
9
]
Let
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}=\operatorname{argmin}_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}})
. Such a
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
always exists by compactness of
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
and continuity of
\psi
. By first order optimality, and convexity of
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
, we have that for all
{\mathbf{C}}\in\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}

\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*}),{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\rangle\leq\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*}),{\mathbf{C}}\rangle\;.

For
{\mathbf{L}}^{*}=\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})
, we have that

{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\in\operatorname{argmin}_{{\mathbf{C}}\in\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}\langle{\mathbf{L}}^{*},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle.

Due to the monotonicity condition on
\psi
the diagonal elements of its gradient
\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})
are positive, and the off-diagonal elements are non-positive, and hence no two columns of
{\mathbf{L}}^{*}
are identical. Thus by a direct application of Lemma
26
, we have that
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\in{\mathcal{C}}
, and moreover
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
is the unique minimizer of
\langle{\mathbf{L}}^{*},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle
over all
{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
.

A.3
Proof of Proposition
10
(Bayes-optimal Classifier for Continuous
\psi,\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{K}
)

Proposition

(Bayes optimal classifier for continuous
\psi,\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{K}
)
.

Let the performance measure
\psi:[0,1]^{d}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and the constraint functions
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{K}:[0,1]^{d}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{R}}
in
OP2
be continuous and bounded. Then there exists
d+1
loss matrices
{\mathbf{L}}^{*}_{1},{\mathbf{L}}^{*}_{2},\ldots,{\mathbf{L}}^{*}_{d+1}
(which can depend on
\psi,\phi_{k}
’s and
D
)
such that an optimal classifier for
OP2
can be expressed as a randomized combination of the deterministic classifiers
h_{1},h_{2},\ldots,h_{d+1}
, where
h_{i}
is optimal for the linear metric given by
{\mathbf{L}}^{*}_{i}
.

{proof}

We first note that
{\mathcal{C}}
is a compact set, and as a result there exists an optimal-feasible solution
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
for
OP2
in
{\mathcal{C}}
. It is straightforward to see that
{\mathcal{C}}
is bounded. To see that
{\mathcal{C}}
is closed,
note that from Proposition
5
, any linear function over
{\mathcal{C}}
is minimized by some confusion matrix in
{\mathcal{C}}
. Since every extreme point is a unique minimizer of a linear function in
{\mathcal{C}}
, every extreme point of
{\mathcal{C}}
is also in
{\mathcal{C}}
. It follows that all convex combinations of the extreme points of
{\mathcal{C}}
are also in
{\mathcal{C}}
and as a result, so are all its limit points.

Now to prove the characterization in the proposition, let us use
{\mathcal{E}}
to denote the set of extreme points of
{\mathcal{C}}
. By Krein–Milman theorem, we have that because
{\mathcal{C}}
is a compact convex set, it is equal to the convex hull of
{\mathcal{E}}

(
Narici and Beckenstein 2010
)
. We further have from Carathéodory’s theorem that any
{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
can be the be expressed as a convex combination of
d+1
points in
{\mathcal{E}}

(
Steinitz 1913
)
. Since
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
is in
{\mathcal{C}}
, we have that there exists
d+1
confusion matrices
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{C}}^{*}_{d+1}\in{\mathcal{E}}
and coefficients
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\Delta_{d+1}
such that
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}=\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\alpha_{i}\,{\mathbf{C}}^{*}_{i}
.

Next, because each
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}_{i}
is an extreme point of
{\mathcal{C}}
, there exists a supporting hyperplane
{\mathbf{L}}^{*}_{i}
for
{\mathcal{C}}
at
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}_{i}
such that
\langle{\mathbf{L}}^{*}_{i},{\mathbf{C}}^{*}_{i}\rangle\leq\langle{\mathbf{L}}^{*}_{i},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle,\,\forall{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
. Moreover, owing to
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
being an extreme point, it is also the unique minimizer of the linear function
\langle{\mathbf{L}}^{*}_{i},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle
. Furthermore, because Proposition
5
tells us that there exists a deterministic classifier
h_{i}
that is optimal for the linear metric
\langle{\mathbf{L}}^{*}_{i},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle
, it follows that
{\mathbf{C}}[h^{*}_{i}]={\mathbf{C}}^{*}_{i}
. Consequently, we have that the randomized classifier
h^{*}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}h_{i}
is an optimal feasible solution for
OP2
, i.e.
{\mathbf{C}}[h^{*}]=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}{\mathbf{C}}[h_{i}]=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}{\mathbf{C}}^{*}_{i}={\mathbf{C}}^{*}
.

A.4
Proof of Theorem
12
(Frank-Wolfe for Unconstrained Problems)

Theorem

((Restated) Convergence of FW algorithm)
.

Fix
\epsilon\in(0,1)
.
Let
\psi:[0,1]^{d}{\rightarrow}[0,1]
be convex, and
\beta
-smooth and
L
-Lipschitz w.r.t. the
\ell_{2}
-norm. Let
\Omega
in Algorithm
1
be a
(\rho,\rho^{\prime},\delta)
-approximate LMO for sample size
m
.
Let
\bar{h}
be a classifier returned by Algorithm
1
when run for
T
iterations. Then with probability
\geq 1-\delta
over draw of
S\sim D^{N}
, after
T={\mathcal{O}}(1/\epsilon)
iterations:

\psi({\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}])\,\leq\,\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\,\psi({\mathbf{C}})\,+\,{\mathcal{O}}\left(\beta\epsilon\,+\,L\rho+\beta\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}\right).

We first prove an important lemma where we bound the approximation error of the linear minimization oracle used in the algorithm. This result coupled with the standard convergence analysis for the Frank-Wolfe method
(
Jaggi 2013
)
will then allow us to prove the above theorem.

Lemma 27
.

Let
\psi:[0,1]^{d}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be convex over
{\mathcal{C}}
, and
L
-Lipschitz and
\beta
-smooth w.r.t. the
\ell_{2}
norm. Let classifiers
\widetilde{h}^{1},\ldots,\widetilde{h}^{T}
, and
h^{0},h^{1},\ldots,h^{T}
be as defined in Algorithm
1
. Then for any
\delta\in(0,1]
, with probability at least
1-\delta
(over draw of
S
from
D^{N}
), we have for all
1\leq t\leq T

\displaystyle\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}]),{\mathbf{C}}[\widetilde{h}^{t}]\rangle\leq\min_{{\mathbf{g}}:{\mathcal{X}}:{\rightarrow}\Delta_{n}}\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}]),{\mathbf{C}}[{\mathbf{g}}]\rangle+L\rho+2\beta\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}.

{proof}

For any
1\leq t\leq T
, let

{\mathbf{g}}^{t,*}\in\operatorname{argmin}_{{\mathbf{g}}:{\mathcal{X}}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{n}}\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}]),{\mathbf{C}}[{\mathbf{g}}]\rangle.
We then have

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{g}}:{\mathcal{X}}:{\rightarrow}\Delta_{n}}\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}]),{\mathbf{C}}[{\mathbf{g}}]\rangle\,-\,\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}]),{\mathbf{C}}[\widetilde{h}^{t}]\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}]),{\mathbf{C}}[{\mathbf{g}}^{t,*}]\rangle\,-\,\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}]),{\mathbf{C}}[\widetilde{h}^{t}]\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}]),{\mathbf{C}}[{\mathbf{g}}^{t,*}]\rangle\,-\,\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{t-1}),{\mathbf{C}}[g^{t,*}]\rangle}_{\text{term}_{1}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\,+\,\underbrace{\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{t-1}),{\mathbf{C}}[g^{t,*}]\rangle\,-\,\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{t-1}),{\mathbf{C}}[\widetilde{h}^{t}]\rangle}_{\text{term}_{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\,+\,\underbrace{\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{t-1}),{\mathbf{C}}[\widetilde{h}^{t}]\rangle\,-\,\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}]),{\mathbf{C}}[\widetilde{h}^{t}]\rangle}_{\text{term}_{3}}.

We next bound each of these terms. We start with
\text{term}_{2}
. For any
1\leq t\leq T
, let
{\mathbf{L}}^{t}
be as defined in Algorithm
1
.
For all
1\leq t\leq T
,

\displaystyle{\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{t-1}),{\mathbf{C}}[{\mathbf{g}}^{t,*}]\rangle-\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{t-1}),{\mathbf{C}}[\widetilde{h}^{t}]\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{t-1})\|_{\infty}(\langle{\mathbf{L}}^{t},{\mathbf{C}}[\widetilde{h}^{t}]\rangle-\langle{\mathbf{L}}^{t},{\mathbf{C}}[{\mathbf{g}}^{t,*}]\rangle)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{t-1})\|_{2}(\langle{\mathbf{L}}^{t},{\mathbf{C}}[\widetilde{h}^{t}]\rangle-\langle{\mathbf{L}}^{t},{\mathbf{C}}[{\mathbf{g}}^{t,*}]\rangle)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle L\rho,

which follows from the property of the LMO (in Definition
11
) and from
L
-Lipchitzness of
\psi
, and holds with probability at least
1-\delta
(over draw of
S
).

Next, for
\text{term}_{1}
, we have by an application of Holder’s inequality

\displaystyle\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}]),{\mathbf{C}}[{\mathbf{g}}^{t,*}]\rangle\,-\,\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{t-1}),{\mathbf{C}}[g^{t,*}]\rangle

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{t-1})-\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}])\big\|_{\infty}\|{\mathbf{C}}[{\mathbf{g}}^{t,*}]\|_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{t-1})-\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}])\big\|_{\infty}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{t-1})-\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}])\big\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\beta\big\|{\mathbf{C}}^{t-1}-{\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}]\big\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\beta\sqrt{d}\big\|{\mathbf{C}}^{t-1}-{\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}]\big\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\beta\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime},

where the third step follows from
\beta
-smoothness of
\psi
; the last step uses the property of the LMO and holds with probability at least
1-\delta
(over draw of
S
).
One can similarly bound
\text{term}_{3}
. We thus have for all
1\leq t\leq T
, with probability at least
1-\delta
(over draw of
S
),

\displaystyle{\max_{{\mathbf{g}}:{\mathcal{X}}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{n}}\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}]),{\mathbf{C}}[{\mathbf{g}}]\rangle-\langle\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}]),{\mathbf{C}}[\widetilde{h}^{t}]\rangle}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle L\rho+2\beta\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime},

as desired.

We are now ready to prove Theorem
12
.

{proof}
[Proof of Theorem
12
]
Our proof shall make use of the standard convergence result for the Frank-Wolfe algorithm for minimizing a convex function over a convex set
(
Jaggi 2013
)
. We will find it useful to first define the following quantity, referred to as the curvature constant in
Jaggi 2013
.

\displaystyle C_{\psi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{{\mathbf{C}}_{1},{\mathbf{C}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}},\gamma\in[0,1]}\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}}\Big(\psi\big({\mathbf{C}}_{1}+\gamma({\mathbf{C}}_{2}-{\mathbf{C}}_{1})\big)-\psi\big({\mathbf{C}}_{1}\big)-\gamma\big\langle{\mathbf{C}}_{2}-{\mathbf{C}}_{1},\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}_{1})\big\rangle\Big).

Also, define two positive scalars
\epsilon_{S}
and
\delta_{\text{apx}}
required in the analysis of
Jaggi 2013
:

\displaystyle\epsilon_{S}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L\rho+2\beta\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}

\displaystyle\delta_{\text{apx}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(T+1)\epsilon_{S}}{C_{\psi}},

where
\delta\in(0,1]
is as in the theorem statement. Further, let the classifiers
\widetilde{h}^{1},\ldots,\widetilde{h}^{T}
, and
h^{0},\ldots,h^{T}
be as defined in Algorithm
1
.
We then have from Lemma
27
that the following holds with probability at least
1-\delta
, for all
1\leq t\leq T
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\psi\left({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}]\right),{\mathbf{C}}[\widetilde{h}^{t}]\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{g}}:{\mathcal{X}}:{\rightarrow}\Delta_{n}}\left\langle\nabla\psi\left({\mathbf{C}}\left[h^{t-1}\right]\right),{\mathbf{C}}\left[{\mathbf{g}}\right]\right\rangle+\epsilon_{S}

(11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\left\langle\nabla\psi\left({\mathbf{C}}\left[h^{t-1}\right]\right),{\mathbf{C}}\right\rangle+\epsilon_{S}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\left\langle\nabla\psi\left({\mathbf{C}}\left[h^{t-1}\right]\right),{\mathbf{C}}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\text{apx}}\frac{2}{T+1}C_{\psi}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\langle\nabla\psi\left({\mathbf{C}}\left[h^{t-1}\right]\right),{\mathbf{C}}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\text{apx}}\frac{2}{t+1}C_{\psi}\;.

Also observe that for the two sequences of iterates given by the confusion matrices of the above classifiers,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}}[h^{t}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{2}{t+1}\right){\mathbf{C}}[h^{t-1}]+\frac{2}{t+1}{\mathbf{C}}[\widetilde{h}^{t}],\;.

(12)

for all
1\leq t\leq T
. Based on (
11
) and (
12
), one can now apply the result of
Jaggi 2013
.

In particular, the sequence of iterates
{\mathbf{C}}[h^{0}],{\mathbf{C}}[h^{1}],\ldots,{\mathbf{C}}[h^{T}]
can be considered as the sequence of iterates arising from running the Frank-Wolfe optimization method to minimize
\psi
over
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
with a linear optimization oracle that is
\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\text{apx}}\frac{2}{t+1}C_{\psi}
accurate at iteration
t
. Since
\psi
is a convex function over the convex constraint set
{\mathcal{C}}
, one has from Theorem 1 in
Jaggi 2013
that the following convergence guarantee holds with probability at least
1-\delta
:

\displaystyle\psi({\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}])~=~\psi({\mathbf{C}}[h^{T}])

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}})+\frac{2C_{\psi}}{T+2}(1+\delta_{\text{apx}})

(13)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}})+\frac{2C_{\psi}}{T+2}+\frac{2\epsilon_{S}(T+1)}{T+2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}})+\frac{2C_{\psi}}{T+2}+2\epsilon_{S}

We can further upper bound
C_{\psi}
in terms of the the smoothness parameter of
\psi
:

\displaystyle C_{\psi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{{\mathbf{C}}_{1},{\mathbf{C}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}},\gamma\in[0,1]}\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}}\Big(\psi\big({\mathbf{C}}_{1}+\gamma({\mathbf{C}}_{2}-{\mathbf{C}}_{1})\big)-\psi\big({\mathbf{C}}_{1}\big)-\gamma\big\langle{\mathbf{C}}_{2}-{\mathbf{C}}_{1},\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}_{1})\big\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{{\mathbf{C}}_{1},{\mathbf{C}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}},\gamma\in[0,1]}\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}}\Big(\frac{\beta}{2}\gamma^{2}||{\mathbf{C}}_{1}-{\mathbf{C}}_{2}||^{2}_{2}\Big)~=\,4\beta\;,

where the second step follows from the
\beta
-smoothness of
\psi
. Substituting back in (
13
), we finally have with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\psi({\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}])

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}})+\frac{8\beta}{T+2}+2\epsilon_{S}~=~\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}})+\frac{8\beta}{T+2}+2L\rho+4\beta\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}.

Setting
T=1/\epsilon
completes the proof.

A.5
Proof of Theorem
13
(GDA for Unconstrained Problems)

Theorem
13
follows from Theorem
17
under the special case of
K=0
. The algorithms for the
constrained and unconstrained case become identical and the same bounds apply with
r=\infty
. Please see Appendix
A.9
for proof of Theorem
17
.

A.6

14
(Ellipsoid For Unconstrained Problems)

Theorem
14
follows from Theorem
18
under the special case of
K=0
. The algorithms for the constrained and unconstrained case become identical and the same bounds apply with
r=\infty
. Please see Appendix
A.10
for proof of Theorem
18
.

A.7
Proof of Theorem
15
(Bisection For Unconstrained Problems)

Theorem
15
follows from Theorem
19
under the special case of
K=0
and
T^{\prime}=1
. Please see Appendix
A.11
for proof of Theorem
19
.

A.8
Proof of Theorem
16
(SplitFW for Constrained Problems)

Theorem

((Restated) Convergence of SplitFW algorithm)
.

Fix
\epsilon>0
. Let
\psi:[0,1]^{d}{\rightarrow}[0,1]
be convex,
\beta
-smooth and
L
-Lipschitz w.r.t. the
\ell_{2}
-norm, and let
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{K}:[0,1]^{d}{\rightarrow}[-1,1]
be convex and
L
-Lipschitz w.r.t. the
\ell_{2}
-norm. Let
\Omega
in Algorithm
5
be a
(\rho,\rho^{\prime},\delta)
-approximate LMO for sample size
N
.
Let
\bar{h}
be a classifier returned by Algorithm
5
when run for
T
iterations with some
\zeta>0
. Let the strict feasibility condition in Assumption
1
hold for radius
r>0
. Then, with probability
\geq 1-\delta
over draw of
S\sim D^{N}
, after
T={\mathcal{O}}(1/\epsilon^{2})
iterations, the classifier
\bar{h}
is near-optimal and near-feasible:

\textbf{Optimality:}~~\psi({\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}])\,\leq\,\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},\phi_{k}({\mathbf{C}})\leq 0,\forall k}\,\psi({\mathbf{C}})\,+\,{\mathcal{O}}\left(\epsilon+\sqrt{\rho^{\textup{{eff}}}}\right);\vskip-5.0pt

\textbf{Feasibility:}~~\phi_{k}({\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}])\,\leq\,{\mathcal{O}}\left(\epsilon+\sqrt{\rho^{\textup{{eff}}}}\right),~\forall k\in[K].

where
\rho^{\textup{{eff}}}=\rho+\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}
and the
{\mathcal{O}}
notation hides constant factors independent of
\rho,\rho^{\prime},T,\epsilon,d,K
for small enough
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
and large enough
T
(or small enough
\epsilon
).

There are two key steps to the proof of this theorem.
First, we show that the use of an approximate LMO in steps 9, 11 of Algorithm
5
does not affect the convergence results by
Gidel et al. 2018
. Specifically, they measure the sub-optimality of an iterate using a duality gap measure.
In Lemma
32
we show that a similar bound on the duality gap can be derived with an approximate LMO over
{\mathcal{C}}
. Second, we use the strict feasibilty assumption to convert a bound on the duality gap into a bound on the sub-optimality of the in problem (
4
) in Lemma
31
.

We will find it useful to first define the following quantities: fat achievable set, dual functions, and the primal and dual gaps.

Definition 28

(Fat achievable set)
.

The set
{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}}
is defined as follows:

{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}}=\left({\mathcal{C}}+B({\mathbf{0}},\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime})\right)\cap\Delta_{d}=\{{\mathbf{C}}+{\mathbf{r}}:{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},\|{\mathbf{r}}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime},{\mathbf{C}}+{\mathbf{r}}\in\Delta_{d}\}.

The set
{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}}
is defined so that the iterates
\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{t}
and
{\mathbf{C}}^{t}
lie within
{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho}^{\prime}
with high probability.

Definition 29

(Dual function)
.

The dual function
f^{\textup{{aug}}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}{\rightarrow}{\mathbb{R}}
is defined as

f^{\textup{{aug}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}},{\mathbf{F}}\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{aug}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{F}},\boldsymbol{\lambda}).

We also use
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),\widehat{\mathbf{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
to denote any arbitrary minimizer of
\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{aug}}}(.,.,\boldsymbol{\lambda})
over
{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}}\times{\mathcal{F}}
. Thus
f^{\textup{{aug}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{aug}}}(\widehat{\mathbf{C}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),\widehat{\mathbf{F}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),\boldsymbol{\lambda})
.
Further, let the maximum value of the dual function be
f^{\textup{{aug}}*}
. By the min-max theorem, we have that

f^{\textup{{aug}}*}=\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}},{\mathbf{F}}\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{aug}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{F}},\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}},{\mathbf{F}}\in{\mathcal{F}}}\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{aug}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{F}},\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}}\cap{\mathcal{F}}}2\psi({\mathbf{C}}).

The last equality follows from the observation that if
{\mathbf{C}}\neq{\mathbf{F}}
then
\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{aug}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{F}},\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\infty
.
Next, let
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}}\cap{\mathcal{F}}
such that

\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})=\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}}\cap{\mathcal{F}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}}).

and let
{\mathcal{W}}^{*}=\operatorname{argmax}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f^{\textup{{aug}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

Definition 30

(Primal and dual gaps)
.

For any
{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}},{\mathbf{F}}\in{\mathcal{F}}
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we define the primal and dual gaps as follows:

\displaystyle\Delta^{(\text{p})}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{F}},\boldsymbol{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{aug}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{F}},\boldsymbol{\lambda})-\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}},{\mathbf{F}}\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{aug}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{F}},\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{aug}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{F}},\boldsymbol{\lambda})-f^{\textup{{aug}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda});

\displaystyle\Delta^{(\text{d})}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})

\displaystyle=f^{\textup{{aug}}*}-f^{\textup{{aug}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=2\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})-f^{\textup{{aug}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),

and define the total gap as
\Delta({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{F}},\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\Delta^{(p)}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{F}},\boldsymbol{\lambda})+\Delta^{(d)}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
.

In the theorems and lemmas below, we will refer to the iterates
{\mathbf{C}}^{t},{\mathbf{F}}^{t},\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{t},\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}^{t}
in the Algorithm
5
. We use the the short-hands
\Delta_{t},\Delta_{t}^{(\text{p})},\Delta_{t}^{(\text{d})}
for representing the same primal and dual gaps evaluated at,
({\mathbf{C}}^{t+1},{\mathbf{F}}^{t+1},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t})
.

We will require the use of Theorem 1 and Corollary 1 from
Gidel et al. 2018
, which we restate below in our notation. We use the following facts to transform their Theorem. The norms of vectors correspond to the
\ell_{2}
-norm unless specified otherwise. We also overload notation and refer to the concatenation of two vectors
{\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{F}}
as
[{\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{F}}]
.

\displaystyle|\psi({\mathbf{C}})+\psi({\mathbf{F}})-\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})-\psi({\mathbf{F}}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq 2L\|[{\mathbf{C}}-{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{F}}-{\mathbf{F}}^{\prime}]\|_{2}

\displaystyle\max\left(\text{eigen-val}\left([I,-I]^{\top}[-I,I]\right)\right)

\displaystyle=2

\displaystyle(\text{diam}({\mathcal{F}}))^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\text{diam}(\Delta_{d})^{2}\leq 2

\displaystyle(\text{diam}({\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}}))^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\text{diam}(\Delta_{d})^{2}\leq 2

\displaystyle(\text{diam}({\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}}\times{\mathcal{F}}))^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4,

where
\|M\|
of a matrix
M
refers to its spectral norm, and
\text{diam}(\mathcal{A})
refers to the diameter of a set
\mathcal{A}
, i.e. the maximum
\ell_{2}
distance between any two elements from the set
\mathcal{A}
.

Theorem

(Restated from
Gidel et al. 2018
)
.

There exists a constant
\alpha>0
such that

\displaystyle f^{\textup{{aug}}*}-f^{\textup{{aug}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{8L_{\zeta}}\min\left\{\alpha^{2}\textup{dist}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\mathcal{W}}^{*})^{2},\alpha L_{\zeta}Z^{2}\textup{dist}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\mathcal{W}}^{*})\right\};

\displaystyle||\nabla f^{\textup{{aug}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})||_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{8L_{\zeta}}\min\left\{\alpha^{2}\textup{dist}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\mathcal{W}}^{*}),\alpha L_{\zeta}Z^{2}\right\};

\displaystyle||\nabla f^{\textup{{aug}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})||_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{8L_{\zeta}}}\min\left\{\sqrt{f^{\textup{{aug}}*}-f^{\textup{{aug}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})},\sqrt{\frac{L_{\zeta}Z^{2}}{2}}\right\},

where
L_{\zeta}=2L+2\zeta
and
dist
represents the standard distance function between a point and a set, i.e.

\text{dist}({\mathbf{x}},\mathcal{A})=\min_{{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}\|
.

We will fix a probability of failure
\delta
throughout the rest of the proof, and assume that the training sample
S
is “good”, in which case the empirical confusion matrix output by the
\Omega
is
\rho^{\prime}
close to the true confusion matrix of the classifier whenever it is called by Algorithm
5
.

We then show below that, if the total gap is low then the resulting classifier is close to optimal and feasible.

Lemma 31
.

Let the assumptions in Theorem
16
hold. Let
g:{\mathcal{X}}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{n}
be a randomized classifier, and
{\mathbf{C}}\in\Delta_{d}
be such that
\|{\mathbf{C}}-{\mathbf{C}}[g]\|_{\infty}\leq\rho^{\prime}
. Let
{\mathbf{F}}\in{\mathcal{F}},\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be such that
\Delta({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{F}},\boldsymbol{\lambda})\leq\tau
and
||{\mathbf{C}}-{\mathbf{F}}||_{2}^{2}\leq\kappa
. We then have:

\displaystyle\psi(C[g])

\displaystyle\leq\min_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{F}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})+\frac{\tau}{2}+(\gamma+L)\sqrt{\kappa}+L\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\mathbf{C}}[g])\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq L(\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}+\sqrt{\kappa}),

where
\gamma=\frac{2L}{r}+\frac{\zeta r}{2L}+\frac{\tau L}{r}
.

{proof}

The second inequality in the lemma trivially follows from the triangle inequality and the
\ell_{2}
Lipschitzness of the constraint functions
\phi_{k}
, i.e. for any
k\in[K]

\displaystyle\phi_{k}({\mathbf{C}}[g])

\displaystyle\leq\phi_{k}({\mathbf{C}})+L\|{\mathbf{C}}-{\mathbf{C}}[g]\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\phi_{k}({\mathbf{C}})+L\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\phi_{k}({\mathbf{F}})+L\|{\mathbf{F}}-{\mathbf{C}}\|_{2}+L\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq L\sqrt{\kappa}+L\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}

We will prove the first inequality below. By construction,
{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}}
. As
\Delta({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{F}},\boldsymbol{\lambda})\leq\tau
, we have

\displaystyle\Delta^{(\text{p})}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{F}},\boldsymbol{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{aug}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{F}},\boldsymbol{\lambda})-\min_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}},{\mathbf{F}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{aug}}}({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{F}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\lambda})\leq\tau

(14)

\displaystyle\Delta^{(\text{d})}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})

\displaystyle=2\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})-\min_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}},{\mathbf{F}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{aug}}}({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{F}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\lambda})\leq\tau

(15)

where
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\in\operatorname{argmin}_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}}\cap{\mathcal{F}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})
. Setting
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{F}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{C}}^{*}
in the second term of Eqn. (
14
):

\psi({\mathbf{C}})+\psi({\mathbf{F}})+\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{T}({\mathbf{C}}-{\mathbf{F}})+\frac{\zeta}{2}\|{\mathbf{C}}-{\mathbf{F}}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})+\tau~.

(16)

The variables
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{F}}^{\prime}
in the second term of (
15
) are set as follows. Let
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{C}}[h]
be a strictly feasible point, i.e.
{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})\leq-r
. Such a
h
exists by Assumption
1
. As the constraint functions
\phi_{k}
are all
L
-Lipschitz w.r.t.
\ell_{2}
norm, a ball of radius
\frac{r}{L}
centered at
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
is a subset of
{\mathcal{F}}
. Further, let
{\mathbf{F}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}+\frac{r}{L||\boldsymbol{\lambda}||}\boldsymbol{\lambda}
. We then have:

2\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})\leq\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})+\psi({\mathbf{F}}^{\prime})-\frac{r\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{2}}{L}+\frac{\zeta r^{2}}{2L^{2}}+\tau.

(17)

This can be reduced to a bound on
||\boldsymbol{\lambda}||_{2}
,

\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{2}\leq\frac{2L}{r}+\frac{\zeta r}{2L}+\frac{\tau L}{r}=\gamma.

(18)

From Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, (
16
) becomes:

\displaystyle\psi({\mathbf{C}})+\psi({\mathbf{F}})

\displaystyle\leq 2\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})+\tau-\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\top}({\mathbf{C}}-{\mathbf{F}})-\frac{\zeta}{2}\|{\mathbf{C}}-{\mathbf{F}}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})+\tau+\gamma\sqrt{\kappa}.

(19)

As
\psi
is
L
-Lipschitz, we have

\psi({\mathbf{C}})-\psi({\mathbf{F}})\leq L\|{\mathbf{C}}-{\mathbf{F}}\|_{2}\leq L\sqrt{\kappa}.

(20)

Adding (
19
) and (
20
) and dividing by 2, we get

\psi({\mathbf{C}})\leq\min_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}}\cap{\mathcal{F}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})+\frac{\tau}{2}+(\gamma+L)\sqrt{\kappa}.

As
{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}}\supseteq{\mathcal{C}}
, and
\psi
is
L
-Lipschitz, we have

\displaystyle\psi(C[{\mathbf{g}}])

\displaystyle\leq\psi({\mathbf{C}})+L\|{\mathbf{C}}-C[{\mathbf{g}}]\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\min_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\rho^{\prime}}\cap{\mathcal{F}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})+\frac{\tau}{2}+(\gamma+L)\sqrt{\kappa}+L\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\min_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{F}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})+\frac{\tau}{2}+(\gamma+L)\sqrt{\kappa}+L\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime},

which completes the proof.

The lemma below bounds the duality gap
\Delta_{t}
and
\|{\mathbf{C}}_{t}-{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\|^{2}
based on the proof of Theorem 2 in
Gidel et al. 2018
. The only difference is the approximate nature of the LMO, that simply contributes an additive factor of
{\mathcal{O}}(\rho+\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime})
to the convergence rate of
{\mathcal{O}}(1/t)
. The proof is highly technical, and we skip it for brevity. The details can be inferred from
Tavker et al. 2020
, which contains the full proof using a different notation.

Lemma 32
.

Let the assumptions in Theorem
16
hold. Let
t_{*}\in[T]
be such that
\bar{h}=h^{t_{*}}
in Algorithm
5
. Let
\Omega
be a
(\rho,\rho^{\prime},\delta)
-approximate LMO. For large enough
T
and
\zeta
, with probability
1-\delta
over draw of
S\sim D^{N}
we have that

\displaystyle\Delta({\mathbf{C}}_{t_{*}},{\mathbf{F}}_{t_{*}},\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t_{*}-1})

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}(\rho+\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime})+\frac{c_{2}}{T};

\displaystyle||{\mathbf{C}}_{t_{*}}-{\mathbf{F}}_{t_{*}}||_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}(\rho+\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime})+\frac{c_{4}}{T},

where
h_{t_{*}},{\mathbf{F}}_{t_{*}},\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t_{*}-1}
are as defined in Algorithm
5
. The constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}
and
c_{4}
are independent of the dimension
d
and number of constraints
K
, approximation constants
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
and iterations
T
. More explicitly,
c_{1}=\frac{4+12\zeta}{a\zeta}
,
c_{2}=16(\beta+2\zeta)(t_{0}+2)
,
c_{3}=\frac{8+24\zeta}{\zeta}\left[1+\frac{2}{a}\right]
,
c_{4}=8\left[32(\beta+2\zeta)\frac{(t_{0}+2)}{a}+\frac{64a(\beta+2\zeta)}{\zeta^{2}}\right]
,
a=\min\left[\frac{2}{\zeta},\frac{\alpha^{2}}{8(\beta+2\zeta)}\right]
, and
t_{0}
is a constant
>0
.

We are now ready to prove Theorem
16
.

{proof}
[Proof of Theorem
16
]
We first note that Lemma
32
can be applied to Lemma
31
setting
\tau=c_{1}\left(\rho+\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}\right)+\frac{c_{2}}{T}
and
\kappa=c_{3}\left(\rho+\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}\right)+\frac{c_{4}}{T}
, with the classifier
g
in Lemma
31
set to the classifer
\overline{h}
returned by Algorithm
5
. For the sake of simplicity, the bound below focuses on the small
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
and large
T
regime. For small enough
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
and large enough
T
, we have
(\gamma+L)\sqrt{\kappa}>\tau+L\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}
, based on the simple argument that for a small enough positive scalar
u
, we have
c\sqrt{u}>u
.
Thus, from the first inequality of Lemma
31
,

\displaystyle\psi({\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}])

\displaystyle\leq\min_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{F}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})+\frac{\tau}{2}+L\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}+(\gamma+L)\sqrt{\kappa}

\displaystyle\leq\min_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{F}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})+2(\gamma+L)\sqrt{\kappa}

\displaystyle\leq\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},\phi_{k}({\mathbf{C}})\leq 0,\forall k}\,\psi({\mathbf{C}})+2(\gamma+L)\left(\sqrt{c_{3}\left(\rho+\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}\right)+\frac{c_{4}}{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},\phi_{k}({\mathbf{C}})\leq 0,\forall k}\,\psi({\mathbf{C}})+2(\gamma+L)\left(\sqrt{c_{3}\left(\rho+\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}\right)}+\sqrt{\frac{c_{4}}{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},\phi_{k}({\mathbf{C}})\leq 0,\forall k}\,\psi({\mathbf{C}})+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\epsilon+\sqrt{\rho+\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}}\right)

By a similar analysis as above, from the second inequality of Lemma
31
, we have for small enough
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
and large enough
T
,

\displaystyle\phi_{k}({\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}])

\displaystyle\leq L\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}+L\sqrt{\kappa}

\displaystyle\leq 2L\sqrt{\kappa}

\displaystyle\leq 2L\left(\sqrt{c_{3}\left(\rho+\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}\right)+\frac{c_{4}}{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathcal{O}}\big(\epsilon+\sqrt{\rho+\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}}\big),

as desired.

A.9
Proof of Theorem
17
(GDA for Constrained Problems)

Theorem

((Restated) Convergence of ConGDA algorithm)
.

Fix
\epsilon\in(0,1)
.
Let
\psi:[0,1]^{d}{\rightarrow}[0,1]
and
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{K}:[0,1]^{d}{\rightarrow}[-1,1]
be convex and
L
-Lipschitz w.r.t. the
\ell_{2}
-norm. Let
\Omega
in Algorithm
6
be a
(\rho,\rho^{\prime},\delta)
-approximate LMO for sample size
N
.
Suppose the strict feasibility condition in Assumption
1
holds for radius
r>0
.
Let the space of Lagrange multipliers
\Lambda=\{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\,|\,\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{2}\leq 2L(1+1/r)\}
,
and
\Xi=\{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{K}\,|\,\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{1}\leq 2/r\}
.
Let
B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\geq\max_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\|_{2}
.
Let
\bar{h}
be a classifier returned by Algorithm
6
when run for
T
iterations,
with step-sizes
\eta=\frac{1}{\bar{L}\sqrt{2T}}

and
\eta^{\prime}=\frac{\bar{L}}{(1+2\sqrt{K})\sqrt{2T}}
, where
\bar{L}=4(1+1/r)L+2/r
.
Then with probability
\geq 1-\delta
over draw of
S\sim D^{N}
, after
T={\mathcal{O}}(K/\epsilon^{2})
iterations:

\psi({\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}])\,\leq\,\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}:\,{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\mathbf{C}})\leq{\mathbf{0}}}\,\psi({\mathbf{C}})\,+\,{\mathcal{O}}\left(L(\epsilon+\rho^{\textup{{eff}}})\right)

\phi_{k}({\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}])\,\leq\,{\mathcal{O}}\left(L(\epsilon+\rho^{\textup{{eff}}})\right),\forall k\in[K],

where
\rho^{\textup{{eff}}}=\rho+\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}
and the
{\mathcal{O}}
notation hides constant factors independent of
\rho,\rho^{\prime},T,d
and
K
.

The proof is an adaptation of the proof of convergence in
Narasimhan et al. 2019
for their oracle-based optimizer (Theorem 3 in their paper), but takes into account three differences:
(i) they consider a generic objective function that is independent of
{\mathbf{C}}
, (ii) they assume that
\phi_{k}
s are monotonic, (iii) they perform a full optimization on
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
instead of gradient-based updates. Moreover, unlike them, we employ exponentiated gradient updates, and derive a better dependence on dimension.

We will first find it useful to first state the following lemma,
which adapts the
proof steps from Lemmas 2, 4 and 6 in
Narasimhan et al. 2019
.

Lemma 33
.

Let
\psi:[0,1]^{d}{\rightarrow}[0,1]
and
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{K}:[0,1]^{d}{\rightarrow}[-1,1]
be convex and
L
-Lipschitz w.r.t. the
\ell_{2}
-norm.
Suppose the strict feasibility condition in Assumption
1
holds for radius
r>0
.
Let
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\in\underset{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}:\,{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\mathbf{C}})\leq{\mathbf{0}}}{\operatorname{argmin}}\,\psi({\mathbf{C}})
, and let

(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})\in\underset{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{K}_{+}}{\operatorname{argmax}}\left\{\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},\,{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta^{d}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\right\}.

Then:

1.

\displaystyle\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})=\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},\,{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{K}_{+}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})=\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{K}_{+}}\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},\,{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}});

2.

\displaystyle\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})=\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Lambda}\min_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\mathcal{L}({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\min_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Lambda}\mathcal{L}({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda}),
for any
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}};

3.

\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}\|_{1}\leq 1/r
;

4.

\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}\|_{2}\leq L(1+1/r)
.

{proof}

For part 1, we begin by writing out the Lagrangian from (
2
):

\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})=\psi({\boldsymbol{\xi}})+\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\mathbf{C}}-{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\rangle+\langle{\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\rangle.

Since
\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}
is convex in
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
and linear in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
, strong duality holds, and we have:

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{K}_{+}}\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},\,{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},\,{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{K}_{+}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},\,{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}:\,{\boldsymbol{\xi}}={\mathbf{C}},\,{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\leq{\mathbf{0}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},:\,{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\mathbf{C}})\leq{\mathbf{0}}}\,\psi({\mathbf{C}})~=\,\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*}).

For part 2, we follow similar steps as part 1 except that it applies to the Lagrangian in (
2
) for the unconstrained problem.

For part 3, recall from our strict feasibility assumption that there exists
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}
such that

\max_{k\in[K]}\phi_{k}({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})\leq-r
for some
r>0
.
It then follows from part 1 that:

\displaystyle\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},\,{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})+\langle{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})\rangle~=\,\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})-r\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}\|_{1}.

We thus have:

\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}\|_{1}\,\leq\,(\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})-\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*}))/r\,=\,1/r.

For part 4,
letting
\omega({\boldsymbol{\xi}})=\psi({\boldsymbol{\xi}})+\langle{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\rangle
,
we note that:

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\min_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})

(21)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\left\{\psi({\boldsymbol{\xi}})+\langle{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\rangle-\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\rangle\right\}+\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\left\{\omega({\boldsymbol{\xi}})-\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\rangle\right\}+\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\omega^{*}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*})+\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\rangle,

where
\omega^{*}
denotes the Fenchel conjugate of
\omega
.
We similarly note that:

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\min_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left\{\min_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\left\{\omega({\boldsymbol{\xi}})-\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\rangle\right\}+\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\rangle\right\}

(22)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left\{-\omega^{*}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})+\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\rangle\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega^{**}({\mathbf{C}}^{*})~=~\omega({\mathbf{C}}^{*}),

where
\omega^{**}
denotes the second Fenchel conjugate of
\omega
.
From (
21
) and (
22
),
its clear that:

\omega({\mathbf{C}}^{*})\,=\,-\omega^{*}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*})+\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\rangle.

An application of the Fenchel-Young inequality then gives us that:

\lambda^{*}=\nabla\omega({\mathbf{C}}^{*})=\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mu^{*}_{k}\nabla\phi_{k}({\mathbf{C}}^{*}).

We can thus bound the norm of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}
as:

\displaystyle\|\lambda^{*}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})\|_{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}|\mu_{k}^{*}|\|\nabla\phi_{k}({\mathbf{C}}^{*})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\nabla\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})\|_{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}\|_{1}\,\max_{k\in K}\|\nabla\phi_{k}({\mathbf{C}}^{*})\|_{2}~=\,L(1+1/r),

which follows from part 2 and the fact that
\psi
and
\phi_{k}
s are Lipschitz w.r.t. the
\ell_{1}
-norm.

{proof}

[Proof of Theorem
17
]
We begin by writing out the Lagrangian from (
2
):

\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})=\psi({\boldsymbol{\xi}})+\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\mathbf{C}}-{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\rangle+\langle{\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\rangle=\underbrace{\psi({\boldsymbol{\xi}})-\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\rangle+\langle{\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\mathbf{C}})\rangle}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}({\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})}+\underbrace{\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\mathbf{C}}\rangle}_{\mathcal{L}_{2}({\mathbf{C}},\boldsymbol{\lambda})}.

Optimality.

To show optimality, note that
\mathcal{L}_{1}
is convex in
\xi
and linear in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
, and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
is linear both in
{\mathbf{C}}
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
.
The use of a
(\rho,\rho^{\prime},\delta)
-approximate LMO to compute
{\mathbf{C}}^{t}
and
h^{t}
at each iteration gives us with probability at least
1-\delta
(over draw of
S
):

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{2}({\mathbf{C}}^{t},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{2}({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t}],\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t})\,+\,\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t}\|_{1}\|{\mathbf{C}}^{t}-{\mathbf{C}}[h^{t}]\|_{\infty}

(23)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t}\|_{\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\left\langle\frac{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t}}{\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t}\|_{\infty}},{\mathbf{C}}\right\rangle\,+\,\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t}\|_{\infty}\rho\,+\,\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t}\|_{1}\rho^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{2}({\mathbf{C}},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t})\,+\,2L(1+1/r)\rho+2L\sqrt{d}(1+1/r)\rho^{\prime}.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{2}({\mathbf{C}},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t})\,+\,\bar{\rho},

where we denote
\bar{\rho}=2L(1+1/r)\rho+2L\sqrt{d}(1+1/r)\rho^{\prime}
.

Next, we apply the classical regret bound guarantee for online gradient
descent

(
Zinkevich 2003
;
Shalev-Shwartz 2011
)
, we have from the sequence of objectives
\mathcal{L}_{1}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t})
’s (where the optimization is over
\xi
). Note that

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\mathcal{L}_{1}({\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\psi({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\|_{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t}\|_{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t}\|_{1}\max_{k}\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\phi_{k}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle L+2L(1+1/r)+2L/r=(3+4/r)L\,\leq\,\bar{L}.

Also note that
\max_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\|_{2}\leq 1
.
So with

\eta=\frac{1}{\bar{L}\sqrt{2T}}
, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{1}({\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t})~\leq~\min_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in[0,1]^{d}}\,\sum_{t=1}^{T}\,\mathcal{L}_{1}({\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t})\,+\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}}{\sqrt{T}}.

(24)

Combining (
23
) and (
24
), we have with probability at least
1-\delta
(over draw of
S
):

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},\,{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in[0,1]^{d}}\,\sum_{t=1}^{T}\,\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t})\,+\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}}{\sqrt{T}}\,+\,\bar{\rho}

(25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},\,{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in[0,1]^{d}}\,\mathcal{L}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}},\bar{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}})\,+\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}}{\sqrt{T}}\,+\,\bar{\rho}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{K}_{+}}\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},\,{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in[0,1]^{d}}\,\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},{\boldsymbol{\lambda}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\,+\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}}{\sqrt{T}}\,+\,\bar{\rho}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}\left\{\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{K}_{+}}\,\min_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in[0,1]^{d}}\,\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},{\boldsymbol{\lambda}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\right\}\,+\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}}{\sqrt{T}}\,+\,\bar{\rho}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}:\,{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\mathbf{C}})\leq{\mathbf{0}}}\psi({\mathbf{C}})\,+\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}}{\sqrt{T}}\,+\,\bar{\rho},

where in the second step
\bar{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t}
and
\bar{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t}
and we use the linearity of
\mathcal{L}
in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
; in the fourth step we use strong duality to interchange the max and min; and the last step follows
from Lemma
33
(part 1).

Similarly, we apply the standard online gradient
ascent
analysis on the sequence of losses
\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t},\cdot,\cdot)
’s, where the optimization is over
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.
Note that

\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\,\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t})\|_{2}=\|{\mathbf{C}}^{t}-{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t}\|_{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t})\|_{2}\leq 1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}

and
\textstyle\left\|\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\\
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{2}\leq 2L(1+1/r)+2/r\leq\bar{L}
(from Lemma
33
, parts 3–4).
So with

\eta^{\prime}=\frac{\bar{L}}{(1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})\sqrt{2T}}
, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t})

(26)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Lambda,\,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Xi}\,\sum_{t=1}^{T}\,\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\,-\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Lambda,\,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Xi}\left\{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\,\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t}],{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\,-\,\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{1}\|{\mathbf{C}}^{t}-{\mathbf{C}}[h^{t}]\|_{\infty}\right\}\,-\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Lambda,\,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Xi}\,\sum_{t=1}^{T}\,\mathcal{L}({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t}],{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\,-\,2L(1+1/r)\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}\,-\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Lambda,\,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Xi}\,\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}],\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\,-\,2L(1+1/r)\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}\,-\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Lambda}\left\{\psi(\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}})+\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}]-\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\rangle\right\}\,+\,\max_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Xi}\,\langle{\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}})\rangle\,-\,2L(1+1/r)\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}\,-\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\min_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in[0,1]^{d}}\left\{\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Lambda}\,\left\{\psi({\boldsymbol{\xi}})+\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}]-{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\rangle\right\}\,+\,\max_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Xi}\,\langle{\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\rangle\right\}\,-\,2L(1+1/r)\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}\,-\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\min_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in[0,1]^{d}}\left\{\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Lambda}\,\left\{\psi({\boldsymbol{\xi}})+\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}]-{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\rangle\right\}\,+\,\langle{\mathbf{0}},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\rangle\right\}\,-\,2L(1+1/r)\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}\,-\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi({\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}])\,-\,2L(1+1/r)\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}\,-\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}},

(27)

where
in the third step, we use the fact that for any
\lambda\in\Lambda
,
\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{2}\leq L
, and the property of the LMO.
In the fourth step, we use

{\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}]=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}{\mathbf{C}}[h^{t}]
and
\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t}
, and use the linearity of
\mathcal{L}
in
{\mathbf{C}}
, and convexity of
\mathcal{L}
in
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
and Jensen’s inequality. In the last step, we apply Lemma
33
(part 2).
The last six steps hold with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Combining (
25
) and (
27
),
we get with probability at least
1-\delta
(over draw of
S
), for any
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime}\in\Xi

\displaystyle\psi({\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}])\leq\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}:\,{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\mathbf{C}})\leq{\mathbf{0}}}\,\psi({\mathbf{C}})\,+\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(2+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}}\,+\,2L(1+1/r)(\rho+2\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}).

Setting

B_{\boldsymbol{\phi}}=\sqrt{K}
and

T=(K+1)/\epsilon^{2}

completes the proof of optimality.

Feasibility.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}^{*},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
be as defined in Lemma
33
.
To show feasibility, combining (
25
) and (
26
), and
interchanging the min and max, we get:

\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Lambda,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Xi}\left\{\psi({\boldsymbol{\xi}})+\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}]-\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\rangle\,+\,\langle{\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}})\rangle\right\}\,\leq\,\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})\,+\,\widetilde{\rho}\,+\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(2+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}},

(28)

where we denote
\widetilde{\rho}=2L(1+1/r)(\rho+2\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime})

Let
k^{\prime}\in\operatorname{argmax}_{k\in[K]}\,\phi_{k}({\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}])
denote the index of the most-violated among the
K
constraints
\phi_{1}({\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}]),\ldots,\phi_{K}({\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}])
. Also let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}

and
\mu^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}=\mu^{*}_{k^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{r}
and
\mu^{\prime}_{k}=\mu^{*}_{k},\forall k\neq k^{\prime}
.
Note that

\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime}\in\Lambda
and

{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime}\in\Xi
.
Substituting
(\mu^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})
into the LHS of (
28
), we have:

\psi(\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}})+\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}]-\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\rangle\,+\,\langle{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\rangle\,+\,\frac{1}{r}\max_{k}\,\phi_{k}(\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}})\,\leq\,\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})\,+\,\widetilde{\rho}\,+\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}},

and we further get:

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},\,{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in[0,1]^{d}}\,\left\{\psi({\boldsymbol{\xi}})+\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\mathbf{C}}-{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\rangle\,+\,\langle{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\rangle\right\}\,+\,\frac{1}{r}\max_{k}\,\phi_{k}(\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})\,+\,\widetilde{\rho}\,+\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}}.

Applying Lemma
33
(part 1),

\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})\,+\,\frac{1}{r}\max_{k}\,\phi_{k}(\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}})\,\leq\,\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})\,+\,\widetilde{\rho}\,+\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}},

giving us for all
k
:

\phi_{k}(\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}})\,\leq\,r\left(\widetilde{\rho}\,+\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}}\right).

(29)

Next set

{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime}={\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}

and

\lambda^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}=\lambda^{*}_{j^{\prime}}+\frac{L(1+1/r)}{\left\|{\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}]-\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\right\|_{2}}({\mathbf{C}}_{j^{\prime}}[\bar{h}]-\bar{\xi}_{j^{\prime}}).

Substituting
(\mu^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})
into the LHS of (
28
), we have:

\displaystyle\psi(\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}})+\langle\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}]-\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\rangle\,+\,\langle{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\rangle\,+\,{L(1+1/r)}\left\|{\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}]-\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})\,+\,\widetilde{\rho}\,+\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}},\hskip 142.26378pt

and again taking a min over
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
and applying Lemma
33
,
we get

\displaystyle{\left\|{\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}]-\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\right\|_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L(1+1/r)}\left(\widetilde{\rho}\,+\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}}\right).

(30)

Combining (
29
) and (
30
), and using the Lipschitz property of
\phi_{k}
, we get for all
k
:

\displaystyle\phi_{k}({\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}])

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle L\left\|{\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}]-\bar{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\right\|_{2}\,+\,r\left(\widetilde{\rho}\,+\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{r(2+r)}{1+r}\left(\widetilde{\rho}\,+\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}}\right)~\leq~r\left(\widetilde{\rho}\,+\,\frac{\sqrt{2}\bar{L}(1+B_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}{\sqrt{T}}\right).

Setting
B_{\boldsymbol{\phi}}=2\sqrt{K}
and

T=(K+1)/\epsilon^{2}

completes the proof of feasibility.

A.10
Proof of Theorem
18
(Ellipsoid for Constrained Problems)

Theorem

((Restated) Convergence of ConEllipsoid)
.

Fix
\epsilon\in(0,1)
.
Let
\psi:[0,1]^{d}{\rightarrow}[0,1]
and
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{K}:[0,1]^{d}{\rightarrow}[-1,1]
be convex and
L
-Lipschitz w.r.t. the
\ell_{2}
norm. Let
\Omega
in Algorithm
6
be a
(\rho,\rho^{\prime},\delta)
-approximate LMO for sample size
N
. Suppose the strict feasibility condition in Assumption
1
holds for radius
r>0
. Let the initial classifier
h^{0}
satisfy this condition, i.e.
{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\mathbf{C}}[h^{0}])\leq-r
and
{\mathbf{C}}[h^{0}]={\mathbf{C}}^{0}
. Let
\bar{d}=d+K
. Let
\bar{h}
be the classifier returned by Algorithm
7
when run for
T>2\bar{d}^{2}\log(\frac{\bar{d}}{\epsilon})
iterations with initial radius
a>2(L+\frac{L+1}{r})
.
Then with probability
\geq 1-\delta
over draw of
S\sim D^{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\textbf{Optimality:}~~\psi({\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}])\,

\displaystyle\leq\,\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}:\,\phi_{k}({\mathbf{C}})\leq 0,\forall k}\,\psi({\mathbf{C}})+\big(4a\big)\epsilon+4a(\rho+2\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime});

\displaystyle\textbf{Feasibility:}~~\phi_{k}({\mathbf{C}}[\bar{h}])\,

\displaystyle\leq\,a(\rho+2\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}),~\forall k\in[K]

In both the constrained and unconstrained versions of the Ellipsoid Algorithm, successive ellipsoids are constructed by obtaining the Löwner-John ellipsoid (JLE), i.e., the minimum volume ellipsoid containing the intersection of the current ellipsoid and a half space obtained by drawing a cutting hyperplane through the current center. This process yields a sequence of ellipsoids with geometrically decreasing volumes. We restate the lemma from
Bubeck 2015
that establishes this fact.

Lemma 34
.

Let the ellipsoid
\mathcal{E}^{0}=\{{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:({\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{c}}_{0})^{\top}\mathbf{H}_{0}^{-1}({\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{c}}_{0})\leq 1)\}
, where
H_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}
is a positive definite matrix and
{\mathbf{c}}_{0},\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Let
(\mathbf{H},{\mathbf{c}})=\textup{{JLE}}(\mathbf{H}_{0},{\mathbf{c}}_{0},{\mathbf{g}})
, where
JLE
refers to the subroutine
3(a)
. Let the ellipsoid
{\mathcal{E}}=\{{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:({\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{c}})^{\top}\mathbf{H}^{-1}({\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{c}})\leq 1)\}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle\supset{\mathcal{E}}^{0}\cap\{{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:{\mathbf{g}}^{\top}({\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{c}}_{0})\geq 0\}

\displaystyle\textup{{vol}}(\mathcal{E})

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\frac{-1}{2d}\right)\hskip 3.00003pt\textup{{vol}}(\mathcal{E}^{0})

where
vol
refers to the standard
d
-dimensional volume.

We will define some functions and variables below that will be useful in our proofs:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})

\displaystyle=\psi({\boldsymbol{\xi}})+\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\top}({\mathbf{C}}-{\boldsymbol{\xi}})+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})

\displaystyle f^{\textup{{con}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})

\displaystyle=\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{0}

\displaystyle:=\{{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d+K}:||{\mathbf{x}}||_{2}\leq a,{\mathbf{x}}_{d+i}\geq 0,\hskip 3.00003pt\forall i\in\{1,2,...,K\}\}

\displaystyle\widehat{f}^{\textup{{con}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})

\displaystyle=f^{\textup{{con}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})-\infty{\mathbf{1}}([\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]\notin\mathcal{R}^{0})

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{argmin}_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\psi({\boldsymbol{\xi}})-\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})

The helper function
\widehat{f}^{\textup{{con}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})
is equal to
f^{\textup{{con}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})
when
[\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]\in\mathcal{R}^{0}
. Let
h^{t}
and
{\mathbf{C}}^{t}
be the iterates in Algorithm
7
. Let
{\mathcal{E}}^{t}
denote the ellipsoid centered at
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t}]
with axes given by the eigen vectors of
{\mathbf{A}}^{t}
, with axes lengths squared given by the corresponding eigenvalues of
{\mathbf{A}}^{t}
, i.e.

{\mathcal{E}}^{t}=\{[\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d+K}:[\boldsymbol{\lambda}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t}]^{\top}{({\mathbf{A}}^{t})}^{-1}[\boldsymbol{\lambda}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t}]\leq 1\}

We abuse notation sometimes in the proof below by interchangeably using the ellipsoid
{\mathcal{E}}^{t}
and its corresponding center, axis matrix
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t}],{\mathbf{A}}^{t}
whenever the context is clear. For example, line 14 of Algorithm
7
can be written compactly as
{\mathcal{E}}^{t+1}=\textup{{JLE}}({\mathcal{E}}^{t},[{\mathbf{C}}^{t}-{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t})])
.

A.10.1
Bounding the Dual Suboptimality of
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t}]

We first prove, that for any iteration
t\in\{0,1,...,T-1\}
, if
[\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]\notin\mathcal{R}^{0}
, then
{\mathcal{E}}^{t+1}\supseteq\{{\mathcal{E}}^{t}\cap\mathcal{R}^{0}\}
. We establish this in the following three lemmas.

Lemma 35
.

If at any iteration
t\in\{0,1,...,T-1\},\hskip 3.00003pt||[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{t},{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{t}]||_{2}>a
, then
{\mathcal{E}}^{t+1}\supseteq\{{\mathcal{E}}^{t}\cap\mathcal{R}^{0}\}

{proof}

Let
t\in\{0,1,...,T-1\}
, such that,
||[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{t},{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{t}]||_{2}>a
. In such a case, the
if
condition (line 5) of algorithm
7
gets invoked and we obtain the new ellipsoid
{\mathcal{E}}^{t+1}
. Due to the
JLE
construction, we get that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{t+1}

\displaystyle\supseteq{{\mathcal{E}}}^{t}\cap\{{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d+K}:({\mathbf{x}}-[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{t},{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{t}])^{\top}(-[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{t},{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{t}]))\geq 0\}

\displaystyle={{\mathcal{E}}}^{t}\cap\{{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d+K}:{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{t},{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{t}]\leq||[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{t},{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{t}]||_{2}^{2}]\}

\displaystyle\supseteq{{\mathcal{E}}}^{t}\cap\mathbf{B}({\mathbf{0}}_{d+K},||[\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{t},\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{t}]||_{2})\supseteq\{{\mathcal{E}}^{t}\cap\mathcal{R}^{0}\}

Thus,
{\mathcal{E}}^{t+1}\supseteq\{{\mathcal{E}}^{t}\cap\mathcal{R}^{0}\}
.

Lemma 36
.

If at any iteration
t\in\{0,1,...,T-1\},\hskip 3.00003pt||[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{t},{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{t}]||_{2}\leq a
, and
{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{t}\ngeq{\mathbf{0}}
, then
{\mathcal{E}}^{t+1}\supseteq\{{\mathcal{E}}^{t}\cap\mathcal{R}^{0}\}

{proof}

Let
t\in\{0,1,...,T-1\}
, such that,
||[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{t},{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{t}]||_{2}\leq a
, while
{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{t}\ngeq{\mathbf{0}}
. In such a case, the
else-if
condition (line 8) of algorithm
7
gets invoked and we obtain the new ellipsoid
{\mathcal{E}}^{t+1}
. Due to the
JLE
construction, we get that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{t+1}

\displaystyle\supseteq{\mathcal{E}}^{t}\cap\{{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d+K}:({\mathbf{x}}-[{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{t},{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{t}])^{\top}([{\mathbf{0}}_{d},\text{pos}(-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t})]))\geq 0\}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}^{t}\cap\{{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d+K}:{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}[{\mathbf{0}}_{d},\text{pos}(-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t})]\geq[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t}]^{\top}[{\mathbf{0}}_{d},\text{pos}(-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t})]

\displaystyle\supseteq{\mathcal{E}}^{t}\cap\{{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d+K}:{\mathbf{x}}_{d+i}\geq 0,\forall i\in{1,2,...,K}\}\supseteq\{{\mathcal{E}}^{t}\cap\mathcal{R}^{0}\}

Thus,
{\mathcal{E}}^{t+1}\supseteq\{{\mathcal{E}}^{t}\cap\mathcal{R}^{0}\}
.

Lemma 37
.

For any iteration
t\in\{0,1,...,T-1\}
of Algorithm
7
, if
[\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]\notin\mathcal{R}^{0}
, then
{\mathcal{E}}^{t+1}\supseteq\{{\mathcal{E}}^{t}\cap\mathcal{R}^{0}\}

{proof}

The result follows directly from Lemmas
35
and
36
.

We would also like to prove that the optimal solution, i.e., the maximizer of
f^{\textup{{con}}}
over
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{K}
indeed lies inside our search space. In our setting, we show in
38
that the maximizer lies inside
\mathcal{R}^{0}

Lemma 38
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})
be a maximizer of
f^{\textup{{con}}}
over
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{K}
. Then
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}]\in\mathcal{R}^{0}

{proof}

From Lemma
33
(parts 3– 4) we have that
\|[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}]\|_{2}\leq L+\frac{L+1}{r}\leq a/2
. Thus:

\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{K}_{+}}f^{\textup{{con}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})=\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{K}_{+}}\widehat{f}^{\textup{{con}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})=\psi({\mathbf{C}}^{*})=\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\mathbf{C}})\leq 0}\psi({\mathbf{C}})

This ensures that
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}]\in\mathcal{R}^{0}

Lemmas
37
and
38
allow us to establish Lemma
39
, which will be required in proving Theorem
44
.

Lemma 39
.

Let
\epsilon\in[0,1]
and
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}]
be any maximiser of
\widehat{f}^{\textup{{con}}}
. Define the convex set
{\mathcal{R}}^{0}_{\epsilon}\subseteq{\mathcal{R}}^{0}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{K}
as

\mathcal{R}^{0}_{\epsilon}:=\{[\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]\in\mathcal{R}^{0}:(1-\epsilon)[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}]+\epsilon[\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]\}.

Let the number of iterations
T
in Algorithm
7
, be such that
T>2(d+K)^{2}\log\left(\frac{2}{\epsilon}\right)
. Then there exists an iteration
t^{*}\in\{0,1,..,T-1\}
such that
\mathcal{R}^{0}_{\epsilon}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}^{t^{*}}
and
\mathcal{R}^{0}_{\epsilon}\not\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}^{t^{*}+1}
and
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t^{*}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t^{*}}]\in\mathcal{R}^{0}
.

{proof}

From Lemma
38
,
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}]\in{\mathcal{R}}^{0}
and thus
{\mathcal{R}}^{0}_{\epsilon}\subseteq{\mathcal{R}}^{0}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}^{0}
. We also have the following by simple geometry and the classic ellipsoid volume reduction result of Lemma
34
.

\displaystyle\textup{{vol}}(\mathcal{R}^{0}_{\epsilon})

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{d+K}\textup{{vol}}({\mathcal{R}}^{0})=\epsilon^{d+K}2^{-K}\textup{{vol}}({\mathcal{E}}^{0})

\displaystyle\textup{{vol}}({\mathcal{E}}^{T})

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\frac{-T}{2(d+K)}\right)\textup{{vol}}({\mathcal{E}}^{0})

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left((d+K)\log\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)\right)\textup{{vol}}({\mathcal{E}}^{0})<\textup{{vol}}(\mathcal{R}^{0}_{\epsilon})

And hence
{\mathcal{R}}^{0}_{\epsilon}\nsubseteq{\mathcal{E}}^{T}
. Clearly, there exists an iteration
t^{*}\in\{0,1,\ldots,T-1\}
such that
{\mathcal{R}}^{0}_{\epsilon}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}^{t^{*}}
but
{\mathcal{R}}^{0}_{\epsilon}\nsubseteq{\mathcal{E}}^{t^{*}+1}
. If
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t^{*}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t^{*}}]\notin{\mathcal{R}}^{0}
, then by Lemma
37
we have that
{\mathcal{E}}^{t^{*}+1}\supseteq{\mathcal{E}}^{t^{*}}\cap{\mathcal{R}}^{0}\supseteq{\mathcal{R}}^{0}_{\epsilon}
, giving a contradiction. Thus
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t^{*}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t^{*}}]\in{\mathcal{R}}^{0}
.

We now prove that
f^{\textup{{con}}}
is a Lipschitz function w.r.t.
\ell_{2}
norm over the domain
\mathcal{R}^{0}
. We will exploit this fact later in the proof for Theorem
44
.

Lemma 40
.

f^{\textup{{con}}}
is a
\sqrt{d+K}
-Lipschitz function w.r.t.
\ell_{2}
norm over the domain
\mathcal{R}^{0}
.

{proof}

The difference
f^{\textup{{con}}}
at

[\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]\in\mathcal{R}^{0}
and
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime}]\in\mathcal{R}^{0}
can be bounded by:

\displaystyle f^{\textup{{con}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})-f^{\textup{{con}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})-\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\max_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})-\mathcal{L}^{\textup{{con}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\left((\boldsymbol{\lambda}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime})^{\top}({\mathbf{C}}-{\boldsymbol{\xi}})+({\boldsymbol{\mu}}-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\left(\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime}\|_{1}\|{\mathbf{C}}-{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\|_{\infty}+\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime}\|_{1}\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\|_{\infty}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime}\|_{1}+\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime}\|_{1}=\|[\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]-[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime}]\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{d+K}\hskip 3.00003pt\|[\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]-[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime}]\|_{2}

Identically,
f^{\textup{{con}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime})-f^{\textup{{con}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\leq\sqrt{d+K}\hskip 3.00003pt\|[\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]-[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime}]\|_{2}
. Thus
|f^{\textup{{con}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime})-f^{\textup{{con}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})|\leq\sqrt{d+K}\hskip 3.00003pt\|[\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]-[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime}]\|_{2}
which concludes the proof.

Recall that we only have access to
(\rho,\rho^{{}^{\prime}},\delta)
-approximate LMO. The sample and approximation errors induced by calls to this approximate LMO must be accounted for. It turns out, that despite having access to only an approximate LMO, we are able to achieve a desirable sub-optimality with probability
1-\delta
over the draw of random sample
S\sim D^{N}
.
The rest of the analysis will only apply for this high probability event
. We now present two lemmas that will be helpful in allowing us to show provided an approximate LMO, the iterates
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t}]
approximately maximize
f^{\textup{{con}}}
and subsequently, we will use these results to convert our dual guarantees into primal guarantees.

Lemma 41
.

Let
t\in\{0,1,...,T-1\}
. Then with probability
1-\delta
(over draw of
S\sim D^{N}
) uniformly for all
t
, such that
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t}]\in\mathcal{R}^{0}
, we have that:

•

{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t}}^{\top}{\mathbf{C}}[h^{t}]\leq\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t}}^{\top}{\mathbf{C}}+a\rho

•

\|{\mathbf{C}}[h^{t}]-{\mathbf{C}}^{t}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime}

•

{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t}}^{\top}{\mathbf{C}}^{t}\leq\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}}{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t}}^{\top}{\mathbf{C}}+a(\rho+\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime})

{proof}

The first two inequalities are simply restatements of the definition of
(\rho,\rho^{\prime},\delta)
-approximate LMO. And the third follows by putting the first two together.

Lemma 42
.

Let
t\in\{0,1,...,T-1\}
and let
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t}]\in\mathcal{R}^{0}
. Then,
[{\mathbf{C}}^{t}-{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t})]
is a
\tau
-supergradient to
\widehat{f}^{\textup{{con}}}
at
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t}]\in\mathcal{R}^{0}
, with
\tau=a(\rho+2\sqrt{d}\rho^{\prime})
, i.e. for all
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{K}
,

\widehat{f}^{\textup{{con}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\leq\widehat{f}^{\textup{{con}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t})+(\boldsymbol{\lambda}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{t})^{\top}({\mathbf{C}}^{t}-{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t})+({\boldsymbol{\mu}}-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{t})^{\top}({\boldsymbol{\phi}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t}))+\tau

{proof}

Fix
[\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]\in\mathcal{R}^{0}
. We have that,

\displaystyle\widehat{f}^{\textup{{con}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})

\displaystyle=\min_{{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\Delta_{d}}\mathcal{L}({\mathbf{C}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{L}({\mathbf{C}}[h^{t}],{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t},\boldsymbol{\lambda},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})
